## Reasoning

Let me analyze the mission carefully:

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score is MINIMIZED
- Nest area: WHITE floor, RED wall side
- Source area: BLACK floor, divided into BLUE half and GREEN half
- Carrying item from one half DECREASES score by 1 (good!)
- Carrying item from the other half INCREASES score by 1 (bad!)
- Need to identify which color (BLUE or GREEN) gives the good items

Since we don't know which is good/bad initially, I need to try both. The key behaviors are:
1. Go to source (BLACK floor) - but specifically the GOOD half
2. Return to nest (WHITE floor) to deliver
3. Avoid the BAD half

**Key perceptions:**
- Floor color: BLACK (source), WHITE (nest), GRAY (middle)
- Wall colors: BLUE, GREEN (identify which half), RED (nest direction)
- Neighbor count

**Strategy:**
- GoToColor BLUE (clr=3) or GREEN (clr=2) to target one specific half
- GoToColor RED (clr=1) to return to nest
- Use floor color transitions to switch behaviors

Let me design FSMs with different strategies:

**Colors in AutoMoDe:** 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

**FSM Design:**

1. **2-state: Explore→GoToBlue, return when WHITE**
   - State 0: Exploration, if BLACK floor → go to state 1 (deliver)
   - State 1: GoToColor RED (nest), if WHITE floor → go to state 0

2. **3-state: Explore, GoToBlue, ReturnToNest**
   - State 0: Explore, BLACK→State1
   - State 1: GoToColor BLUE, BLACK→State2 (but already black... hmm)
   
Let me think differently. The robots need to:
- Find the source (go to BLACK)
- Pick from the CORRECT half (approach BLUE or GREEN wall)
- Return to nest (go to RED wall / WHITE floor)

**FSM 1 (3-state, target BLUE):**
- S0: Exploration (rwm), if BLACK floor → S1
- S1: GoToColor BLUE (clr=3), if WHITE floor → S2 (delivered)
- S2: GoToColor RED (clr=1) to return... wait, need to go back to source

Actually simpler: 
- S0: GoToColor BLUE, if WHITE → S1
- S1: GoToColor RED, if BLACK → S0

**Let me design 14 machines:**

FSM 4-14: variations

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to know which machines performed best. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and the designs I submitted in Round 2, then create a diverse population.

## Reasoning

**Mission recap:** Score is minimized. One wall color (BLUE or GREEN) gives good items (score -1), the other gives bad items (score +1). We need to identify and exploit the good half.

**Key insight:** Since we don't know which color is good, we must try both BLUE-targeting and GREEN-targeting strategies. The evolutionary process will select whichever works.

**Design principles:**
1. **Collect phase:** GoToColor BLUE or GREEN (target specific half)
2. **Return phase:** GoToColor RED (nest wall) or triggered by WHITE floor
3. **Transitions:** BLACK floor = at source, WHITE floor = at nest

**For round 3, I'll include:**
- Survivors of both BLUE and GREEN strategies (elitism)
- Variations with different probabilities
- More complex 3-4 state machines with richer transitions
- Machines that use GRAY floor as intermediate signal
- Mixed strategies with neighbor-based transitions

Let me carefully construct all 14 machines with correct syntax:

**State indexing reminder for destinations:**
- 2 states, from S0: D=0→S1; from S1: D=0→S0
- 3 states, from S0: D=0→S1, D=1→S2; from S1: D=0→S0, D=1→S2; from S2: D=0→S0, D=1→S1
- 4 states, from S0: D=0→S1, D=1→S2, D=2→S3; from S1: D=0→S0, D=1→S2, D=2→S3; from S2: D=0→S0, D=1→S1, D=2→S3; from S3: D=0→S0, D=1→S1, D=2→S2

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Since I haven't received explicit scores from the simulator, I need to reason carefully about what should work best for this mission.

**Key insight I may have been missing:** The robots start scattered in the center. The source is at one end (BLACK floor), nest at the other (WHITE floor, RED wall). The BLUE and GREEN walls are behind the two halves of the source. One color gives -1 score (good), one gives +1 (bad).

**Critical realization:** GoToColor behavior moves toward the nearest visible object of that color. When a robot is near the source, it can see BLUE or GREEN walls. When near the nest, it sees the RED wall. The GoToColor RED strategy should reliably bring robots to the nest.

**What I should improve:**
1. The transition from source-collecting to returning should be more reliable
2. Need both BLUE and GREEN variants since we don't know which is good
3. Should also try: while returning (GoToColor RED), transition on WHITE floor back to collecting
4. The GRAY floor transition could help - when on GRAY heading toward source, keep going

**New ideas to explore:**
- Use GRAY floor trigger to switch from return-mode to collect-mode midway
- Try Exploration with higher turning rates to better cover source area
- 4-state machines with more nuanced behavior
- Try combinations: GoToBlue in collect, GoAwayRed in source (stay longer)
- Use neighbor counts to coordinate

**Topology I'm most confident about:**
- 2-state: GoToColor(BLUE/GREEN) → [WHITE floor] → GoToColor(RED) → [BLACK floor] → back
- 3-state with exploration phase for better source-finding

Let me create 14 machines, mixing BLUE and GREEN strategies, various topologies:

I've been running for 4 rounds without feedback on scores. Let me think more carefully about what might be going wrong and what should work best.

**Critical analysis:**

The core loop must be:
1. Go to source (BLACK floor) - specifically the GOOD half
2. Pick up item (automatic when entering BLACK)
3. Return to nest (WHITE floor) - deliver item
4. Repeat

**Potential issues with my designs:**
- GoToColor BLUE/GREEN: robots chase the wall behind the source. But once they're IN the source (on BLACK floor), they might already have the item and should leave. The transition should be on BLACK floor → start returning, not WHITE.
- Wait: "Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item" - so just being on BLACK gives item. Then "entering the nest with an item delivers it" - so need to reach WHITE.
- So the cycle is: on BLACK (have item) → go to WHITE (deliver) → go back to BLACK (get item) → repeat

**Revised strategy:**
- State A: GoToColor BLUE (navigate to blue half) - when on BLACK floor, switch to return
- State B: GoToColor RED (navigate to nest) - when on WHITE floor, switch to collect

The BLACK floor trigger for returning is key! Not WHITE as the collect trigger.

**Let me reconsider transitions:**
- In collect state: transition on BLACK floor (arrived at source) → go to return state
- In return state: transition on WHITE floor (arrived at nest) → go to collect state

This is actually what I had for FSM1 and FSM2. Let me make sure these are correct and push stronger variations.

**Another issue:** GoToColor moves toward nearest visible object. In the middle of the arena (GRAY), robots may not see any colored walls. Exploration might be needed to traverse the middle.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Five rounds in with no score feedback means I need to reason carefully. The core 2-state loop (GoToColor target → BLACK floor → GoToColor RED → WHITE floor → repeat) is theoretically sound. Both BLUE and GREEN variants must be kept since we don't know which half is beneficial. I'll maintain all proven topologies and add richer multi-transition variants to improve robustness.

## Reasoning

Six rounds without score feedback. I'll stay disciplined: keep all topologies that are theoretically correct and diversify more aggressively. The two key unknowns are (1) which color is good (BLUE or GREEN) and (2) whether GoToColor reliably navigates across the gray middle zone. I'll add exploration-first variants and also try using GRAY floor as an intermediate trigger to help robots cross the middle more reliably.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Seven rounds without score feedback forces me to reason purely from first principles. My designs have been theoretically sound but perhaps I'm missing something fundamental. Let me reconsider.

The problem: one half gives -1 (good), one gives +1 (bad). We don't know which. The evolutionary optimizer should have found the good color by now if my FSMs work. Perhaps the issue is that GoToColor needs the colored wall to be visible - if walls are far away or occluded, robots may wander. Also, GoAwayColor from the bad wall naturally pushes toward the good wall.

Key insight I may be underweighting: GoAwayColor GREEN pushes robots toward BLUE half, and GoAwayColor BLUE pushes toward GREEN half. These are complementary strategies to GoToColor. I should include both orientations strongly.

I'll keep the best theoretical designs (2-state and 3-state with floor triggers) and add genuinely new topologies: starting from GoToColor RED (go to nest first to establish position), using GRAY floor as a waypoint signal, and trying attraction/repulsion behaviors for the middle crossing.

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 3 --cle0 5 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 0.9 --s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 1 --cle1 6 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 0.9

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Eight rounds in. The optimizer has been running on my designs but I have no score feedback. I need to think about what might be fundamentally wrong or what I haven't tried.

**Fresh analysis:** The scoring says one half decreases score by 1, the other increases by 1. Since score is minimized, we want the decreasing half. The walls behind the halves are BLUE and GREEN. We don't know which is which, so we need both strategies. The evolutionary optimizer should select the better one.

**What might be failing:** GoToColor navigates toward the nearest visible colored object. In a closed arena, BLUE and GREEN walls are at the far end. RED wall is at the nest end. The robot starting in the middle might see both BLUE and GREEN walls, and GoToColor BLUE would move toward BLUE. This should work.

**New idea I haven't tried enough:** What if I try GoToColor with colors 4 (yellow), 5 (magenta), 6 (cyan)? No - those aren't arena colors. The arena has RED (nest wall), BLUE and GREEN (source walls). So clr=1 (red), clr=2 (green), clr=3 (blue) are the relevant ones.

**Critical new thought:** Maybe I should try machines that do NOT use GoToColor RED for return, but instead use GoToColor with some other mechanism. Or use Exploration for the return trip - robots might naturally drift back. Or try Repulsion from source walls to return to nest.

**Another new idea:** Use GoAwayColor BLUE for returning (if BLUE is the bad side, avoid it = go toward nest). But GoAway from source walls would push toward nest walls (RED), which is what we want for delivery.

Let me try genuinely new topologies:
- GoAwayColor(source wall) for return phase instead of GoToColor RED
- Pure exploration machines
- Machines with GRAY floor as intermediate waypoint
- Machines starting from GoToColor RED (establish at nest first)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

This is the final round. I need to make my best 12 machines. Without score feedback, I must rely on theory. The core insight is: one wall color (BLUE=3 or GREEN=2) gives good items, the other bad. GoToColor that color to collect (transition on BLACK floor), GoToColor RED to return (transition on WHITE floor).

The simplest 2-state machines are theoretically optimal if navigation works. I'll field both BLUE and GREEN variants strongly, plus the best 3-state and 4-state variants. I'll also try GoAwayColor variants since pushing away from the bad wall is equivalent to moving toward the good half. I'll maximize diversity across these final 12 slots to ensure at least one strong performer gets found.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
